Custom proxy configuration
To inspect, filter, or log sandbox traffic with your own tooling, replace the built-in sandbox proxy with a proxy you run on the same machine.
To route sandbox traffic through a corporate proxy elsewhere on your network, set
HTTPS_PROXY
instead, as the
Corporate proxy
entry under
Network isolation
describes. That way, Claude Code’s allowlist still applies.
To direct sandboxed commands to your proxy, set the localhost ports it listens on in
sandbox settings
:
{
"sandbox"
: {
"network"
: {
"httpProxyPort"
:
8080
,
"socksProxyPort"
:
8081
}
}
}
If you set a port and also set
HTTPS_PROXY
or
HTTP_PROXY
, Claude Code doesn’t forward what sandboxed commands send to your proxy on to the proxy those variables name. To reach a corporate proxy, configure your own proxy to forward to it.
Which files can set a port depends on your other sandbox settings. The first case that matches applies:
allowManagedDomainsOnly
is on
: managed settings only
The sandbox is
admin-required
, or a
narrower network lock
applies
: managed settings,
--settings
, and user settings
Otherwise
: any settings file
Claude Code ignores a port set anywhere else. Before v2.1.285, any settings file could set a port.
Once either port applies, your proxy is responsible for filtering everything sent to it. Claude Code’s own network controls, such as
allowedDomains
,
deniedDomains
,
strictAllowlist
, approval prompts, and the
local-address check
, stop applying to that traffic. A sandboxed command can connect to either proxy, so if you set only one port, Claude Code’s domain lists on the other proxy don’t limit what the command reaches through yours.
​
Troubleshooting
Some commands fail inside the sandbox even though they work outside it. Find the heading that matches your symptom or error message.
If your organization’s sandbox is
admin-required
, Claude Code ignores the settings these fixes name in a project’s settings files, so save them in
~/.claude/settings.json
, where they apply in every project. If a fix still has no effect, your organization’s managed settings may set that key.
A fix that adds an
excludedCommands
pattern removes the sandbox from the commands the pattern matches. See
what an excluded command can do
.
​
Commands fail with a host-not-allowed error
Many CLI tools need to reach specific hosts. Approve the host when prompted, or add it to
allowedDomains
. If your organization locks the allowlist with
allowManagedDomainsOnly
, there’s no prompt, so ask your administrator to add the host.
​
jest
hangs or fails
watchman
is incompatible with the sandbox. Run
jest --no-watchman
instead.
​
Go-based CLIs fail TLS verification on macOS
Tools such as
gh
,
gcloud
, and
terraform
may fail TLS verification under
Seatbelt
. To run these tools outside the sandbox, add a pattern for each tool, such as
gh *
, to
excludedCommands
. The tool then runs with your full access and its stored credentials. If you are using
httpProxyPort
with a MITM proxy and custom CA, set
enableWeakerNetworkIsolation
to
true
instead.
​
open
,
osascript
, or browser-based auth flows fail with error
-600
on macOS
The sandbox blocks Apple Events by default. Set
allowAppleEvents
to
true
in your user, managed, or CLI settings to allow them. Claude Code ignores this key in project settings.
Enabling
allowAppleEvents
removes code-execution isolation, since sandboxed commands can then launch other applications unsandboxed with no user prompt, and can send AppleScript commands to running applications, subject to the macOS automation-consent prompt (TCC). Alternatively, add a pattern such as
open *
to
excludedCommands
. Each
open
call then goes through the permission flow, and
open
can launch any file or app, including one Claude wrote.
​
docker
commands fail
docker
is incompatible with the sandbox. Take the
docker
commands you need out of the sandbox with an
excludedCommands
pattern such as
docker compose *
.
Run commands outside the sandbox with
excludedCommands
explains what an excluded
docker
command can reach. A narrower pattern takes fewer commands out of the sandbox.
​
Chromium or rootless Podman fails inside the sandbox on Linux
On Linux and WSL2, commands in the sandbox can’t create user namespaces by default, so tools that rely on them fail:
Chromium exits with
No usable sandbox!
unshare -U true
prints
unshare: unshare failed: Operation not permitted
. With
allowAllUnixSockets
on, it can print
No space left on device
instead
Rootless Podman prints
cannot clone: Operation not permitted
, or stops earlier with
read-only file system
on one of its own folders
In a session with
CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB
set to
1
, start Chromium or Electron with
--no-sandbox
, and run any other tool yourself outside Claude Code. In that session,
every command runs sandboxed
, and the settings that the other fixes below name have no effect.
Each fix works for the tools it names and gives up some isolation:
For Chromium or Electron, start the browser with
--no-sandbox
. The Claude Code sandbox stays as it is, but the browser runs web content without its own sandbox.
For rootless Podman, Buildah, or a script or test command that runs a browser or
unshare
, add a pattern to
sandbox.excludedCommands
, such as
podman *
. Commands that match run outside the sandbox with your full access, and Claude can edit a script in your working directory before running it. The pattern has to match the command Claude runs, so for a browser, match the test command rather than the browser.
To keep Chromium’s or Electron’s own sandbox, or to run a nested bubblewrap or
unshare
, set
sandbox.allowNestedUserNamespaces
to
true
in
~/.claude/settings.json
, not the project’s
.claude/settings.json
. Requires Claude Code v2.1.305 or later. A command that begins with
unshare
stays sandboxed whatever
excludedCommands
pattern you list, because
unshare
can start any other program. This setting alone isn’t enough for rootless Podman or Buildah, which the sandbox stops in other ways too.
If you set
allowNestedUserNamespaces
to
true
, you turn off a security boundary: code from a sandboxed command can end up running outside the sandbox.
Don’t rely on any of the sandbox’s restrictions, including
protected paths
and
credentials.files
entries, to stop a malicious command. In
auto-allow mode
, that command can run without a permission prompt. If your organization has a security team, check with them before you turn this setting on.
​
pbcopy
,
xclip
, or
wl-copy
doesn’t update the clipboard
The
pbcopy
,
xclip
, and
wl-copy
clipboard utilities can fail to reach the system clipboard from inside the sandbox, in which case the text piped to them doesn’t arrive.
To put Claude’s output on your clipboard, ask Claude to print it in its response, then run
/copy
.
/copy
writes to the clipboard from the Claude Code process rather than from a sandboxed command.
When Claude pipes text to one of these tools, adding the tool to
excludedCommands
doesn’t take that call out of the sandbox on its own.
​
A git command fails with
unable to unlink old
git merge
,
git checkout
, and similar commands fail with
unable to unlink old
when they need to replace a file the sandbox denies writes to. On Linux and WSL2 the error ends with
Read-only file system
. The file can be in one of these places:
Under a
protected path
such as
.claude/skills
Under one of your
denyWrite
entries
Outside the directories the sandbox lets commands write to at all
After the failure, Claude may
offer to rerun the command outside the sandbox
. Approve that retry, or run the git command yourself in another terminal. If you’ve set
allowUnsandboxedCommands
to
false
, Claude can’t offer the retry, so run the command yourself.
​
A folder named
objects
or
refs
disappears after a command
A top-level
objects
or
refs
folder that appeared during the session can be
deleted after a sandboxed command ends
. It doesn’t matter whether a command, Claude, or you created it. To keep a folder, leave something in it. If a
HEAD
file or symlink has been at the top level during the session, move it away, then restart every Claude Code session open there.
Before v2.1.307, the folder was deleted even with something in it and no
HEAD
. On an earlier version,
update Claude Code
.
​
Bubblewrap fails to start inside a container
In an unprivileged container,
bubblewrap
can’t mount a fresh
/proc
filesystem, so sandboxed commands fail with a
bwrap
error such as
Can't mount proc on /newroot/proc: Operation not permitted
. Set
enableWeakerNestedSandbox
to
true
so the sandbox bind-mounts the container’s existing
/proc
instead. Only use this setting when the outer container already provides the isolation boundary you need, since the setting exposes process information to sandboxed commands that a fresh
/proc
mount would hide.
​
0-byte read-only files appear at
.claude
settings paths, and “Yes, and don’t ask again” doesn’t save
On Linux and WSL2, the sandbox holds a write denial on a file that doesn’t exist yet by creating a 0-byte read-only placeholder there while a sandboxed command runs. The sandbox removes the placeholder afterward.
A placeholder stays behind in cases such as these:
The session is killed before that cleanup runs, for example by SIGKILL
The session exits while a sandboxed command is still running, such as a background command
Later sessions bind the placeholders read-only again on every start, so a settings write such as saving a permission choice fails at a path where a placeholder remains.
Run
claude doctor
in your terminal to list the leftover placeholder files. The
Sandbox mask files left behind at write-deny paths
warning names some of them and counts the rest. Delete each file with
rm
while no other Claude Code session is running in that project. Before v2.1.257, Claude Code left the same placeholders behind when a session was killed, without flagging them.
​
git
over SSH fails with the sandbox on
On macOS,
git fetch
,
git pull
, and
git push
against an SSH remote fail inside the sandbox even when the host is allowed. On Linux and WSL2, they work once the host is allowed. Claude Code tunnels git’s SSH connection through the
sandbox proxy
, and the macOS tunnel can’t authenticate to that proxy.
On Linux and WSL2, check these if the connection still fails:
The host is allowed on port 22
: an
allowedDomains
entry with no port, such as
"git.example.com"
, covers it
Your corporate proxy permits port 22
: if your network requires an upstream proxy, the tunnel goes through it too
The key is readable as a file
: the sandbox can block the
ssh-agent
socket, and a
denyRead
or
credentials
entry for
~/.ssh
hides your key files
On macOS, switch the remote to HTTPS, which needs HTTPS credentials such as a personal access token:
git
remote
set-url
origin
https://git.example.com/example-org/example-repo.git
If you have to keep the SSH remote, take git’s network commands out of the sandbox with
excludedCommands
:
{
"sandbox"
: {
"excludedCommands"
: [
"git fetch *"
,
"git pull *"
,
"git push *"
]
}
}
These entries match
git push origin main
. A call that adds a
cd
, uses
git -C
, or contains a command substitution stays sandboxed. The excluded git commands can reach any host, not only the ones in
allowedDomains
.
Plain
ssh
,
scp
, and
rsync
over SSH fail for the reason
the database client entry
gives.
​
A database client or other non-HTTP tool fails to reach an allowed host
A tool that ignores the proxy environment variables can’t connect from inside the sandbox, even to a host in
allowedDomains
. A sandboxed command has
no direct route to the network
, so a tool that opens its own connection fails. Most database drivers, plain
ssh
, and tools that use UDP behave this way.
The failure looks like a network or name resolution error:
macOS
:
Operation not permitted
, or a name resolution error such as
Could not resolve host
Linux and WSL2
:
Network is unreachable
, or a name resolution error such as
Temporary failure in name resolution
A tool that uses the proxy fails differently when its host isn’t allowed. You get a network prompt, or the tool receives a
403
response from the proxy.
To let the tool connect, run the command that needs it outside the sandbox with
excludedCommands
. This example excludes one script and adds an
ask rule
so that you approve each run:
{
"sandbox"
: {
"excludedCommands"
: [
"python scripts/load_orders.py *"
]
},
"permissions"
: {
"ask"
: [
"Bash(python scripts/load_orders.py *)"
]
}
}
The script runs with your full access, and Claude can edit a script that’s inside your working directory, so review it when the prompt appears.
​
A command fails to reach a server on localhost
By default, a sandboxed command can’t connect directly to a server that’s running on your machine outside the sandbox, such as a dev server or a database in a container. What you can change depends on your platform:
macOS
: set
network.allowLocalBinding
to
true
. Sandboxed commands can then listen on network ports and connect to any port on localhost, which includes every other service listening there. A localhost service that doesn’t require authentication, such as a debugger, can then act for the command outside the sandbox, and a command that listens on a non-loopback address accepts connections from other machines
Linux and WSL2
: a sandboxed command’s
localhost
is private to that command. The command can listen on a port and reach servers it started itself. A direct connection to
localhost
or
127.0.0.1
doesn’t reach servers on the host, and
allowLocalBinding
has no effect. Run the command that needs the host’s server outside the sandbox with
excludedCommands
, where it has no filesystem or network limits. For connections that go through the sandbox proxy, see
Hostnames that resolve to local addresses
This example turns the setting on for macOS:
{
"sandbox"
: {
"network"
: {
"allowLocalBinding"
:
true
}
}
}
An
allowedDomains
entry for
localhost
applies to connections that go through the proxy, so it doesn’t change a direct connection. Claude Code sets
NO_PROXY
for sandboxed commands so that they connect to
localhost
directly instead of through the proxy. The entry also exposes every port on your machine’s localhost to a command that does use the proxy. For a development hostname that points at
127.0.0.1
, see
An allowed hostname is refused with
resolved to a loopback address
.
​
An allowed hostname is refused with
resolved to a loopback address
The sandbox proxy refuses an allowed hostname that
resolves to a local address
, which affects development names such as
myapp.test
that point at
127.0.0.1
. The command sees a
403
response whose body names the kind of address, such as
Connection to myapp.test blocked: resolved to a loopback address
.
Add the IP address the name resolves to alongside the hostname in
allowedDomains
, each with the port your server listens on:
{
"sandbox"
: {
"network"
: {
"allowedDomains"
: [
"myapp.test:3000"
,
"127.0.0.1:3000"
]
}
}
}
An IP address entry with no port lets sandboxed commands reach every service listening on that address.
Before v2.1.284, the proxy connected to whatever address an allowed hostname resolved to.
​
Commands are refused with
Sandbox is required
If you require the sandbox with
sandbox.failIfUnavailable
and it fails in one of these ways after Claude Code starts, each sandboxed command Claude runs is refused with the message shown. The session keeps running, and the refused command doesn’t run unsandboxed:
Sandbox is required but failed to initialize: <reason>
: Claude Code started, but the sandbox then failed to initialize. Take the step the message gives:
Restart to retry.
or
Fix the sandbox settings to retry
.
Sandbox is required (sandbox.failIfUnavailable) and stopped being available in this session: <reason>
: on Linux or WSL2, the sandbox started and Claude Code then found a dependency missing. Fix what the reason names, such as
installing a missing dependency
, then restart Claude Code.
Excluded commands and commands you type yourself keep running, and Claude can’t retry a refused command unsandboxed:
Excluded commands
: commands that match an
excludedCommands
entry keep running outside the sandbox. When one writes to a
filesystem.allowWrite
path outside your working directory, that write goes through the regular permission flow. Under an
admin-required sandbox
, Claude Code ignores entries from the repository’s settings
Commands you type at the
!
shell-mode prompt
: keep running outside the sandbox. In a
background session
, they’re refused too
Unsandboxed retries
: Claude Code ignores the
dangerouslyDisableSandbox
parameter of an
unsandboxed retry
In a Linux session with
CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB
set, every command runs sandboxed, so excluded commands and commands you type are refused too.
​
/sandbox
fails with
Sandbox settings are overridden by a higher-priority configuration
/sandbox
prints
Error: Sandbox settings are overridden by a higher-priority configuration and cannot be changed locally.
instead of opening its panel when a higher
settings level
sets
sandbox.enabled
,
sandbox.autoAllowBashIfSandboxed
, or
sandbox.allowUnsandboxedCommands
. The panel saves your choices to
.claude/settings.local.json
, and a value saved there can’t override those levels.
Managed settings and
--settings
rank above local settings. To see which of them this session loaded, run
/status
and read the
Setting sources
line:
Command line arguments
: if you started Claude Code with
--settings
, check whether the file or JSON you passed sets one of those keys. If it does, change the value there, or start Claude Code again without those keys.
Enterprise managed settings
: your organization’s managed settings are loaded. If they set one of those keys, you can’t change that key from
/sandbox
or from any settings file you control, so ask your administrator.
​
/sandbox
prints
Sandbox settings not changed
This line means
/sandbox
couldn’t save the choice you made in its panel to your project’s
.claude/settings.local.json
:
✗ Sandbox settings not changed: /home/you/project/.claude/settings.local.json couldn't be updated (EACCES). Start Claude Code in a project folder you can write to.
If the reason in parentheses is
EACCES
,
EPERM
, or
EROFS
, start Claude Code in a project folder you can write to, then run
/sandbox
again in that session. If the reason begins with
Invalid JSON syntax in settings file
, correct the JSON in your project’s
.claude/settings.local.json
, then run
/sandbox
again.
If no writable project folder is available to you, turn the sandbox on from your user settings instead. Add this to
~/.claude/settings.json
:
{
"sandbox"
: {
"enabled"
:
true
}
}
This turns on
auto-allow mode
. For regular permissions mode, also set
sandbox.autoAllowBashIfSandboxed
to
false
. The change takes effect in your running session at the next shell command Claude runs, and the
Mode
tab of
/sandbox
then marks the mode in effect.
​
Limitations
Sandboxing reduces risk but is not a complete isolation boundary. Review the limitations below before relying on it as a hard security control.
​
Security limitations
Network filtering
: the sandbox restricts which domains processes can connect to. By default the built-in proxy does not terminate or inspect TLS on outbound traffic, so the contents of encrypted connections are not examined. The experimental
network.tlsTerminate
setting terminates TLS at the proxy for
mask
credential substitution
but does not add content filtering. You are responsible for ensuring that only trusted domains are allowed in your policy.
Allowing broad domains such as
github.com
can create paths for data exfiltration. Because the proxy makes its allow decision from the client-supplied hostname without inspecting TLS, code running inside the sandbox can potentially use
domain fronting
or similar techniques to reach hosts outside the allowlist. If your threat model requires stronger guarantees, configure a
custom proxy
that terminates TLS and inspects traffic, and install its CA certificate inside the sandbox. Stronger TLS-aware network isolation is an active area of development.
Privilege escalation via Unix sockets
: the
allowUnixSockets
configuration can inadvertently grant access to system services that could lead to sandbox bypasses. For example, allowing access to
/var/run/docker.sock
effectively grants access to the host system through the Docker socket. Consider carefully any Unix sockets that you allow through the sandbox.
Filesystem permission escalation
: overly broad filesystem write permissions can enable privilege escalation attacks. Allowing writes to directories containing executables in
$PATH
, system configuration directories, or user shell configuration files such as
.bashrc
or
.zshrc
can lead to code execution in different security contexts when other users or system processes access these files.
Linux sandbox strength
: the Linux implementation provides strong filesystem and network isolation but includes an
enableWeakerNestedSandbox
mode that enables it to work inside Docker environments without privileged namespaces. This option considerably weakens security and should only be used when additional isolation is otherwise enforced.
Apple Events on macOS
: the macOS sandbox blocks Apple Events by default. The
allowAppleEvents
setting lifts this restriction so tools such as
open
and
osascript
work, but it removes code-execution isolation: sandboxed commands can launch other applications unsandboxed with no user prompt, and can send AppleScript commands to running applications, subject to the per-app macOS automation-consent prompt (TCC). It is only honored from user, managed, or CLI settings. Project settings cannot enable it.
User namespaces on Linux and WSL2
: by default, sandboxed commands can’t create user namespaces. If you set
allowNestedUserNamespaces
to
true
, you turn off a security boundary: code from a sandboxed command can end up running outside the sandbox. Before you turn this setting on, read the warning in
Chromium or rootless Podman fails inside the sandbox on Linux
.
​
Scope
The sandbox isolates shell commands and their child processes.
What runs outside the sandbox
lists the tools and helper processes it doesn’t cover. Computer use and subagents relate to the sandbox as follows:
Computer use
: when Claude opens apps and controls your screen, it runs on your actual desktop rather than in an isolated environment. Per-app permission prompts gate each application. See
computer use in the CLI
or
computer use in Desktop
.
Subagents
:
subagents
run in the same process as the parent session and use the same sandbox configuration. Bash commands inside a subagent are sandboxed when sandboxing is enabled in the parent session.
Background sessions
: a
background session
runs in its own process, and its Bash commands are sandboxed when
its settings
enable sandboxing.
A boundary around the whole process
: to put the processes in
What runs outside the sandbox
behind a boundary too, run Claude Code (local mode) inside the
sandbox runtime
with a network allowlist limited to the hosts you approve, or in the
dev container
with its firewall script. For the background service and the sessions it hosts, see
Run Claude Code behind a corporate launcher
.
Mods
: a
mod
is a plugin that runs its own code inside Claude Code, and a process that a mod starts runs outside the sandbox. See
What a mod can reach
.
Effective sandboxing requires both filesystem and network isolation. Without network isolation, a compromised agent could exfiltrate sensitive files like SSH keys. Without filesystem isolation, whether from a permissive policy or from
disabling the filesystem layer
, a compromised agent could backdoor system resources to gain network access. When you widen the defaults, check that an
allowWrite
path, a broad
allowedDomains
entry, or an
excludedCommands
exception does not undo a restriction on the other side.
​
See also
Sandbox environments
: compare the built-in sandbox with dev containers, containers, and VMs
Security
: security features and best practices
Permissions
: permission configuration and access control
All settings
: every settings key
CLI reference
: command-line options

`,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#network-isolation`,children:`network isolation`}),` controls which domains they can reach. If you mask only the secret, requests are signed with a placeholder the proxy can’t detect, so they fail at AWS.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`Claude Code links the conventional `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`}),`, `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`}),`, and `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`AWS_SESSION_TOKEN`}),` variables into one credential automatically when you mask their whole values. When one of these requests is signed with a masked pair’s placeholder, the proxy fails it rather than forward a broken signature. Requests signed with unmasked credentials aren’t affected. Use `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/settings-reference#sandbox-credentials-sigv4`,children:_jsx(_components.code,{children:`credentials.sigv4`})}),`, which requires Claude Code v2.1.224 or later, to forward one of these request forms instead. Claude Code also pre-allows domains from `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`WebFetch(domain:...)`}),` allow rules, as described in `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#permission-rules`,children:`Permission rules`}),`.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Strict allowlist`}),`: if you set `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/settings-reference#sandbox-network-strictallowlist`,children:_jsx(_components.code,{children:`strictAllowlist`})}),` to `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`true`}),` in user, managed, or CLI `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`--settings`}),` settings, Claude Code denies sandboxed commands access to any host outside the allowlist instead of prompting. The allowlist is `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowedDomains`}),` plus domains from `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`WebFetch(domain:...)`}),` allow rules, or only the managed settings entries when `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowManagedDomainsOnly`}),` is set. `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#locks-that-apply-without-an-admin-required-sandbox`,children:`Locks that apply without an admin-required sandbox`}),` covers a repository’s entries. Claude Code enforces this for sandboxed commands only; in-process tools such as `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`WebFetch`}),` still follow their `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#permission-rules`,children:`permission rules`}),`. Setting it in a repository’s `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`.claude/settings.json`}),` or `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`.claude/settings.local.json`}),` has no effect. Requires Claude Code v2.1.219 or later.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Managed lockdown`}),`: if `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/settings-reference#sandbox-network-allowmanageddomainsonly`,children:_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowManagedDomainsOnly`})}),` is set in managed settings, non-allowed domains are blocked automatically instead of prompting, and only `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowedDomains`}),` and `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`WebFetch(domain:...)`}),` allow rules from managed settings are honored.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Corporate proxy`}),`: when your network requires outbound traffic to go through a corporate proxy, set `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`HTTPS_PROXY`}),`, `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`HTTP_PROXY`}),`, and `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`NO_PROXY`}),` as `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/network-config#proxy-configuration`,children:`proxy configuration`}),` describes, in the `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`env`}),` block of your settings so that `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/network-config#set-network-variables-in-settings-not-the-shell`,children:`background agents`}),` get them too, or in the environment you launch Claude Code from. Claude Code enforces the domain allowlist and then tunnels allowed connections through that upstream proxy. `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`http://`}),` and `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`https://`}),` proxy URLs work, with basic authentication in the URL if you need it.`]}),`\n`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`In a `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`WebFetch(domain:...)`}),` rule, the sandbox honors two wildcard forms: a leading `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`*.`}),`, such as `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`*.example.com`}),`, and a bare `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`*`}),`. The bare `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`*`}),` form requires Claude Code v2.1.186 or later. A wildcard in any other position, such as `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`WebFetch(domain:example.*)`}),`, still matches fetches but has no effect on sandboxed commands.`]}),`\n`,_jsx(Note,{children:_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`The built-in proxy enforces the allowlist based on the requested hostname and, by default, does not terminate or inspect TLS traffic. a sandboxed command can’t connect directly to a server that’s running on your machine outside the sandbox, such as a dev server or a database in a container. What you can change depends on your platform:`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.ul,{children:[`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`macOS`}),`: set `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/settings-reference#sandbox-network-allowlocalbinding`,children:_jsx(_components.code,{children:`network.allowLocalBinding`})}),` to `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`true`}),`. Sandboxed commands can then listen on network ports and connect to any port on localhost, which includes every other service listening there. A localhost service that doesn’t require authentication, such as a debugger, can then act for the command outside the sandbox, and a command that listens on a non-loopback address accepts connections from other machines`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Linux and WSL2`}),`: a sandboxed command’s `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`localhost`}),` is private to that command. The command can listen on a port and reach servers it started itself. A direct connection to `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`localhost`}),` or `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`127.0.0.1`}),` doesn’t reach servers on the host, and `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowLocalBinding`}),` has no effect. addresses`})]}),`\n`]}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`This example turns the setting on for Review the limitations below before relying on it as a hard security control.`}),`\n`,_jsx(Heading,{level:`3`,id:`security-limitations`,children:`Security limitations`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.ul,{children:[`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Network filtering`}),`: the sandbox restricts which domains processes can connect to. By default the built-in proxy does not terminate or inspect TLS on outbound traffic, so the contents of encrypted connections are not examined. The experimental `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/settings-reference#sandbox-network-tlsterminate`,children:_jsx(_components.code,{children:`network.tlsTerminate`})}),` setting terminates TLS at the proxy for `,_jsxs(_components.a,{href:`#mask-credentials`,children:[_jsx(_components.code,{children:`mask`}),` credential substitution`]}),` but does not add content filtering. You are responsible for ensuring that only trusted domains are allowed in your policy.`]}),`\n`]}),`\n`,_jsx(Warning,{children:_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`Allowing broad domains such as `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`github.com`}),` can create paths for data exfiltration. Because the proxy makes its allow decision from the client-supplied hostname without inspecting TLS, code running inside the sandbox can potentially use `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domain_fronting`,children:`domain fronting`}),` or similar techniques to reach hosts outside the allowlist. If your threat model requires stronger guarantees, configure a `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#custom-proxy-configuration`,children:`custom proxy`}),` that terminates TLS and inspects traffic, and install its CA certificate inside the sandbox. Stronger TLS-aware network isolation is an active area of development.`]})}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.ul,{children:[`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Privilege escalation via Unix sockets`}),`: the `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowUnixSockets`}),` configuration can inadvertently grant access to system services that could lead to sandbox bypasses. For example, allowing access to `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`/var/run/docker.sock`}),` effectively grants access to the host system through the Docker socket. Consider carefully any Unix sockets that you allow through the sandbox.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Filesystem permission escalation`}),`: overly broad filesystem write permissions can enable privilege escalation attacks. Allowing writes to directories containing executables in `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`$PATH`}),`, system configuration directories, or user shell configuration files such as `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`.bashrc`}),` or `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`.zshrc`}),` can lead to code execution in different security contexts when other users or system processes access these files.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Linux sandbox strength`}),`: the Linux implementation provides strong filesystem and network isolation but includes an `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`enableWeakerNestedSandbox`}),` mode that enables it to work inside Docker environments without privileged namespaces. This option considerably weakens security and should only be used when additional isolation is otherwise enforced.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Apple Events on macOS`}),`: the macOS sandbox blocks Apple Events by default. The `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowAppleEvents`}),` setting lifts this restriction so tools such as `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`open`}),` and `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`osascript`}),` work, but it removes code-execution isolation: sandboxed commands can launch other applications unsandboxed with no user prompt, and can send AppleScript commands to running applications, subject to the per-app macOS automation-consent prompt (TCC). It is only honored from user, managed, or CLI settings. Project settings cannot enable it.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`User namespaces on Linux and WSL2`}),`: by default, sandboxed commands can’t create user namespaces. If you set `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/settings-reference#sandbox-allownestedusernamespaces`,children:_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowNestedUserNamespaces`})}),` to `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`true`}),`, you turn off a security boundary: code from a sandboxed command can end up running outside the sandbox. Before you turn this setting on, read the warning in `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#chromium-or-rootless-podman-fails-inside-the-sandbox-on-linux`,children:`Chromium or rootless Podman fails inside the sandbox on Linux`}),`.`]}),`\n`]}),`\n`,_jsx(Heading,{level:`3`,id:`scope`,children:`Scope`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`The sandbox isolates shell commands and their child processes. `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#what-runs-outside-the-sandbox`,children:`What runs outside the sandbox`}),` lists the tools and helper processes it doesn’t cover. Computer use and subagents relate to the sandbox as follows:`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.ul,{children:[`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Computer use`}),`: when Claude opens apps and controls your screen, it runs on your actual desktop rather than in an isolated environment. Per-app permission prompts gate each application. See `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/computer-use`,children:`computer use in the CLI`}),` or `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/desktop#let-claude-use-your-computer`,children:`computer use in Desktop`}),`.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Subagents`}),`: `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/sub-agents`,children:`subagents`}),` run in the same process as the parent session and use the same sandbox configuration. Bash commands inside a subagent are sandboxed when sandboxing is enabled in the parent session.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Background sessions`}),`: a `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/agent-view`,children:`background session`}),` runs in its own process, and its Bash commands are sandboxed when `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/agent-view#settings-and-provider`,children:`its settings`}),` enable sandboxing.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`A boundary around the whole process`}),`: to put the processes in `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#what-runs-outside-the-sandbox`,children:`What runs outside the sandbox`}),` behind a boundary too, run Claude Code (local mode) inside the `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/sandbox-environments#sandbox-runtime`,children:`sandbox runtime`}),` with a network allowlist limited to the hosts you approve, or in the `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/devcontainer`,children:`dev container`}),` with its firewall script. For the background service and the sessions it hosts, see `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/corporate-launcher`,children:`Run Claude Code behind a corporate launcher`}),`.`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.strong,{children:`Mods`}),`: a `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/plugins/mods/overview`,children:`mod`}),` is a plugin that runs its own code inside Claude Code, and a process that a mod starts runs outside the sandbox. See `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/plugins/mods/overview#what-a-mod-can-reach`,children:`What a mod can reach`}),`.`]}),`\n`]}),`\n`,_jsx(Warning,{children:_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`Effective sandboxing requires both filesystem and network isolation. Without network isolation, a compromised agent could exfiltrate sensitive files like SSH keys. Without filesystem isolation, whether from a permissive policy or from `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`#disable-filesystem-isolation`,children:`disabling the filesystem layer`}),`, a compromised agent could backdoor system resources to gain network access. When you widen the defaults, check that an `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowWrite`}),` path, a broad `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`allowedDomains`}),` entry, or an `,_jsx(_components.code,{children:`excludedCommands`}),` exception does not undo a restriction on the other side.`]})}),`\n`,_jsx(Heading,{level:`2`,id:`see-also`,children:`See also`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.ul,{children:[`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/sandbox-environments`,children:`Sandbox environments`}),`: compare the built-in sandbox with dev containers, containers, and VMs`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/security`,children:`Security`}),`: security features and best practices`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/permissions`,children:`Permissions`}),`: permission configuration and access control`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/settings-reference`,children:`All settings`}),`: every settings key`]}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.li,{children:[_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/en/cli-reference`,children:`CLI reference`}),`: command-line options`]}),`\n`]})]})}function MDXContent(props={}){const{wrapper:MDXLayout}={..._provideComponents(),...props.components};return MDXLayout?_jsx(MDXLayout,{...props,children:_jsx(_createMdxContent,{...props})}):_createMdxContent(props)}return{default:MDXContent};function _missingMdxReference(id,component){throw new Error(`Expected `+(component?`component`:`object`)+\" `\"+id+\"` to be defined: you likely forgot to import, pass, or provide it.\")}","frontmatter":{},"scope":{"config":{},"pageMetadata":{"title":"Configure the sandboxed Bash tool","sidebarTitle":"Bash sandbox","description":"Restrict the files and network hosts Claude Code's shell commands can reach with the built-in sandbox.
